Hello Professor! 

I was born in Brest Belarus so I am pretty aware about some aspects of WW2 including USSR perspective and casualties, national way of thinking, social problems like alcoholism rates and live longevity. I was raised in Russia through leaving in 4 different regions, plus also I have roots in Donbas Ukraine spending there every summer during my school holidays.

I started my own research on Feb 24 2022. Was shocked by the way things were unfolding so I decided to find the roots of the conflict and understand each side perspectives, plans and narratives. I live in United States so I was able to experience and observe it from the inside, having conversations with locals, doing debates etc. My goal was to find middle ground within pro west / pro Ukraine / pro EU / pro Russia propaganda and media lies, which I was able to achieve.

### **@offdabean** | 👍 102 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=Ugz6x0WS2C2_JyCHG_x4AaABAg)

It is worth noting that Stalin and the Soviet Union tried multiple times throughout the 30s to establish a united front against Europe’s new/rising fascist governments with France, Britain, Poland, etc, with those countries obviously reneging or outright ignoring these treaties. From the failed Eastern Pact (1934) and the ineffective Franco-Soviet Treaty (1935) to Western non-intervention in the Spanish Civil War, it became clear to the Soviets that Western powers were appeasing Hitler and European fascism at best, or angling for it to strike and expand eastward towards the USSR. With that said, the Molotov-Ribbentrop Pact was as much a pact for the Soviets to have breathing room of what was clearly looking like Western acquiescence to German fascism (the Munich Agreement which the Soviets were excluded from and Nazi Germany’s later invasion of Czechoslovakia). While plenty castigated the Soviets for making a pact with what was both materially and ideologically their sworn enemies, the use of the pact for breathing room cannot be understated, especially in light of an increasingly belligerent and eastward-expanding Third Reich.

> **@pietrocavanni1500** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=UgyU98skEDQ4bmaN9q54AaABAg.AJBVhDU0zvOAOAmNJFmQYa)  
>
> This video is full of fallacies and factual errors — appeal to low-credibility authority (Wikipedia), cherry-picking, strawman arguments, and speculation.
  
> Below is a summary based on serious historiographical sources:
  
> 
  
> **Hitler was not “tricked” by Stalin:** since *Mein Kampf* (1925), Hitler had advocated *Lebensraum* (expansion to the East). The invasion of the USSR was ideological and premeditated, not manipulated.
  
> → Ian Kershaw, *Hitler: A Biography*; Richard Overy, *Russia’s War*.
  
> 
  
> **The starvation of Soviet POWs** was not a “logistical failure” but a deliberate racial policy (*Commissar Order*, *Generalplan Ost*).
  
> → US Holocaust Memorial Museum, “Commissar Order”.
  
> 
  
> **Churchill opposed Hitler** for imperial and strategic reasons, not out of “personal opinion.”
  
> → Geoffrey Roberts, *Stalin’s Wars* (Yale UP, 2006).
  
> 
  
> **American industry in Nazi Germany:** investments were motivated by profit and market access, not ideological affinity.
  
> → Antony Sutton, *Wall Street and the Rise of Hitler* (1976).
  
> 
  
> **Lend-Lease was not a “deception”:** the U.S. armed the USSR to wear down the Wehrmacht and open the Western Front.
  
> → Albert Weeks, *Russia’s Life-Saver* (2004).
  
> 
  
> **The “Stalin as Okhrana agent” theory is false:** no document confirms it; both Montefiore and Khlevniuk reject this myth.
  
> → Simon Sebag Montefiore, *Young Stalin* (2007); Oleg Khlevniuk, *Stalin: New Biography of a Dictator* (2015).
  
> 
  
> **Stalin condemned the cult of personality** and attempted to resign multiple times; he did not rule by personal decree but within the Party structure.
  
> → Grover Furr, *Khrushchev Lied* (2011), ch. 4;
  
> → Domenico Losurdo, *Stalin: História e Crítica de uma Lenda Negra* (Revan, 2010).
  
> 
  
> **The Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact** was a tactical maneuver following the failure of anti-fascist fronts (1934–1938).
  
> → Domenico Losurdo, *War and Revolution* (Boitempo, 2015);
  
> → Geoffrey Roberts, *The Unholy Alliance* (1989).
  
>

>
> This video is full of fallacies and factual errors — appeal to low-credibility authority (Wikipedia), cherry-picking, strawman arguments, and speculation.
  
> Below is a summary based on serious historiographical sources:
  
> 
  
> **Hitler was not “tricked” by Stalin:** since *Mein Kampf* (1925), Hitler had advocated *Lebensraum* (expansion to the East). The invasion of the USSR was ideological and premeditated, not manipulated.
  
> → Ian Kershaw, *Hitler: A Biography*; Richard Overy, *Russia’s War*.
  
> 
  
> **The starvation of Soviet POWs** was not a “logistical failure” but a deliberate racial policy (*Commissar Order*, *Generalplan Ost*).
  
> → US Holocaust Memorial Museum, “Commissar Order”.
  
> 
  
> **Churchill opposed Hitler** for imperial and strategic reasons, not out of “personal opinion.”
  
> → Geoffrey Roberts, *Stalin’s Wars* (Yale UP, 2006).
  
> 
  
> **American industry in Nazi Germany:** investments were motivated by profit and market access, not ideological affinity.
  
> → Antony Sutton, *Wall Street and the Rise of Hitler* (1976).
  
> 
  
> **Lend-Lease was not a “deception”:** the U.S. armed the USSR to wear down the Wehrmacht and open the Western Front.
  
> → Albert Weeks, *Russia’s Life-Saver* (2004).
  
> 
  
> **The “Stalin as Okhrana agent” theory is false:** no document confirms it; both Montefiore and Khlevniuk reject this myth.
  
> → Simon Sebag Montefiore, *Young Stalin* (2007); Oleg Khlevniuk, *Stalin: New Biography of a Dictator* (2015).
  
> 
  
> **Stalin condemned the cult of personality** and attempted to resign multiple times; he did not rule by personal decree but within the Party structure.
  
> → Grover Furr, *Khrushchev Lied* (2011), ch. 4;
  
> → Domenico Losurdo, *Stalin: História e Crítica de uma Lenda Negra* (Revan, 2010).
  
> 
  
> **The Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact** was a tactical maneuver following the failure of anti-fascist fronts (1934–1938).
  
> → Domenico Losurdo, *War and Revolution* (Boitempo, 2015);
  
> → Geoffrey Roberts, *The Unholy Alliance* (1989).
  
> 
  
> **The video mixes speculation and revisionism without primary sources.**
  
> Recommended readings: Grover Furr (*Blood Lies*), Domenico Losurdo (*Stalin*), Geoffrey Roberts (*Stalin’s Wars*), and Richard Overy (*Russia’s War*).

### **@caspery.6293** | 👍 67 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=Ugzn0uSGED9mgQ9mbht4AaABAg)

This lecture is mostly accurate, but a few notable inaccuracies, and Stalin gets way too much credit. 

1. He did not "trick Hitler into invading him," Hitler himself outlined in Mein Kampf back in the 1925 that his ultimate goal was to create a 'Greater Germanic Reich' via eastward expansion. There seems to be quite a bit of Nazi whitewashing in this video in general. i.e., Soviet POWs starving was not just because of "logistical failures," they were ideologically considered to be subhuman, and other POWs were treated far better. 

2. Churchill's stance on Hitler was not "personal," it was both ideological and strategic. Ideologically, he was a British conservative and he despised dictatorship, but it was Germany actively disrupting the balance of power in Europe, which threatened the British Empire's dominant position.

3. Americans were not considered 'Nordic people' by the Nazis, they were actually considered weak and racially impure because of diversity and immigration.

4. American industrialists invested in Germany because it was still one of the richest countries, and Nazis allowed private investment. They did not invest because of ideological agreement, but mainly because of profit and access to a major European economy. 

5. The Americans were not "duped" by Stalin into arming the USSR. This was a calculated strategic move to keep the USSR in the war, wear down the German Army, and to eventually force Germany to fight on two fronts so D-Day can happen in the West.

6. A lot of stuff like the Great Purge being beneficial and Stalin allowing Barbarossa to happen is not supported by evidence and is pure speculation.

But on your main point, I completely agree: Stalin was probably among the most skilled statesmen to ever live, and the underlying reason was his enormous understanding of power and strategy.

Without Stalin's planned invasion of Europe, and Germany taking preemptive action, and all the resulting casualties, Russia would probably have a population closer to the United States than what it has today. ### **@ChainsOfBelief** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=UgxJXiXpaUIQhPacbLB4AaABAg)

Love the lecture as usual. Stalin being called the "greatest man to ever live," is a wild thing for a non-Russian politician to say lol. I think starting out by defining what you mean by "greatest" would help me understand more. The Professor here seems to use a definition suggesting how powerful, impactful, or historically consequential a person's legacy was relative to their early life humble beginnings. That definition works great for Stalins case. It also seems like a very tailor-made definition to support the claim. I understand the need to remove morality from the equation when talking about how power works in the world, but when talking about how "great" someone was, it seems wrong to dismiss the moral consequences of someone's actions. I usually measure the greatness of a person by how they improved lives. Stalins legacy is riddled with widespread suffering directly caused by his policies.

### **@pietrocavanni1500** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=UgzF6iE0JqQ7AcaJuYR4AaABAg)

This video is full of fallacies and basic historical distortions, reproducing Wikipedia-style narratives and second-hand anti-communist literature without any academic rigor. Below are some key points:



Strawman Fallacy / Nazi Whitewashing

Hitler was not “tricked” by Stalin into invading the USSR — he had already stated in *Mein Kampf* (1925) that *Lebensraum* (“expansion to the East”) was the Reich’s ultimate goal. The video distorts this by absolving Nazi ideology and shifting responsibility onto Stalin. Soviet POWs did not die due to “logistical failures,” but because of a deliberate racial policy (*Generalplan Ost*).

→ Source: Ian Kershaw, *Hitler: A Biography* (W.W. Norton, 2008); Richard Overy, *Russia’s War* (Penguin, 1997).



Cherry-Picking / Selective Reading of Churchill and the West

Churchill did not hate Hitler for “personal reasons,” but for imperial and geopolitical ones — the European balance of power was being threatened.

→ Source: Geoffrey Roberts, *Stalin’s Wars* (Yale University Press, 2006).



Factual Error on the “American Race” and Nazism

Nazism viewed Americans as racially degenerate due to miscegenation and immigration. There is no basis for the claim that they were considered “Nordic.”

→ Source: Claudia Koonz, *The Nazi Conscience* (Harvard University Press, 2003).



Economic, Not Ideological, Interests

American investments in Nazi Germany were driven by profit, not ideological affinity. Major corporations also operated in the USSR before the war, reflecting capitalist pragmatism rather than a “communist conspiracy.”

→ Source: Antony C. Sutton, *Wall Street and the Rise of Hitler* (1976) — even though anti-Soviet, Sutton himself demonstrates it was about financial, not ideological, interests.



False Causality: Lend-Lease and Alleged “American Deception”

American support to the USSR (Lend-Lease) was strategic, not the result of Stalin’s “manipulation.” Washington needed to keep the Red Army active on the Eastern Front to pave the way for the Allied landings.

→ Source: Albert L. Weeks, *Russia’s Life-Saver: Lend-Lease Aid to the U.S.S.R. in World War II* (Lexington Books, 2004).



Speculative Fallacy (“Stalin Provoked the Invasion”)

There is no evidence that Stalin “wanted” the invasion. On the contrary, he was in full mobilization, trying to buy time until military parity with Germany was achieved.

→ Source: David M. Glantz, *Stumbling Colossus: The Red Army on the Eve of World War* (University Press of Kansas, 1998);

→ Grover Furr, *Blood Lies: The Evidence that Every Accusation Against Joseph Stalin and the Soviet Union in Timothy Snyder’s Bloodlands Is False* (2014), ch. 7.



On the “Cult of Personality”

Stalin publicly condemned personal cults on several occasions (see his 1930 speech and 1938 letters). He even attempted to resign more than once (notably in 1926 and 1952), but the Central Committee refused.

→ Source: Grover Furr, *Khrushchev Lied* (2011), ch. 4;

→ Domenico Losurdo, *Stalin: História e Crítica de uma Lenda Negra* (Revan, 2010), chs. 5 and 7.



On Soviet Foreign Policy in the 1930s

The USSR attempted to form anti-fascist fronts with France, Poland, and the UK (Eastern Pact of 1934, Franco-Soviet Treaty of 1935). All failed due to Western sabotage and the appeasement policy toward Hitler (Munich, 1938). The Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact was a tactical retreat to gain time, not an “alliance with Hitler.”

→ Source: Geoffrey Roberts, *The Unholy Alliance: Stalin’s Pact with Hitler* (Indiana University Press, 1989);

→ Domenico Losurdo, *War and Revolution: The Myth of the Anti-Totalitarian West* (Boitempo, 2015).



Lack of Primary Sources

The entire video relies on Wikipedia and YouTube, ignoring declassified Soviet documents, diplomatic telegrams, Comintern correspondence, and Stalin’s works available at the Marxists Internet Archive. This lack of serious historical method amounts to an appeal to low-credibility authority.



Conclusion

The video mixes conspiracy theories with soft Nazi revisionism and vulgar liberalism.

Stalin was not “an irrational tyrant,” but a Marxist-Leninist statesman of complex strategic rationality — acknowledged even by non-communist historians such as Roberts and Overy.

→ See also:

Domenico Losurdo, *Stalin: História e Crítica de uma Lenda Negra* (Revan, 2010).

Grover Furr, *Blood Lies* (2014).

Geoffrey Roberts, *Stalin’s Wars* (2006).

### **@Leo_oe_I**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QtqfIjK1GAc&lc=UgyoPFFQm6uBylI0j3l4AaABAg)

Too many people in this comment section seem to be interpreting "great" as "morally great" and using it as an opportunity to praise Stalin as a desirable or righteous leader, rather than in the historically pivotal, brutally effective kind of great of Julius Caesar, Genghis Khan, or Philip of Macedon that Professor Xueqin CLEARLY means based on the contents of his lecture. Julius Caesar killed millions of Gauls, for instance, and Genghis Khan slaughtered orders of magnitude more while setting in motion events that would end the Islamic Golden Age. They were "great" in the sense that they were skilled, larger than life figures who did what few could. He's arguing that Stalin was _this_ kind of figure: a monster, but a "great" monster. Commenters seem to be jumping on a band-wagon and either explicitly white-washing Stalin's reputation with their glowing admiration or hinting at their desire to do so, but if you watch the whole video, that's not what the professor is doing here. He details all the horrendous crimes of Joseph Stalin, the betrayal of his compatriots, and the countless deaths of civilians as part of his attempt to argue why he was so proficient at what he did and accomplished, not to endorse it. It's certainly not a dystopia I'd want to live in, and Stalin clearly shaped the revolution in his own cultish image, but he _did_ also industrialize the Soviet Union at a lightning pace, oversee the defeat of Nazi Germany, and transform a backwards country into a military super-power. And of course that inevitably leads to the question, "But at what cost?" It's not about justifying or downplaying his crimes, but Professor Xueqin is setting morality aside to assess these accomplishments, not argue that we should revive Stalinism.
